My hands get sweaty, and I just feel this instant rush of panic before I've even had time to really think it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar panic or fear in situations other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's... it's just elevators. I don't get that feeling anywhere else. I'm totally fine in other small spaces or whatever, and heights don't bother me. It's literally just elevators that do this to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid any social situations because of fear of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, my job is literally training people, so I'm pretty comfortable talking in front of groups or being evaluated. It's really just the elevator thing that's the problem. I don't worry about people judging me or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators related to being in a confined or enclosed space, or is it something specific about elevators?\n\nPatient: It's definitely just the elevators themselves. I mean, I know an elevator in a regular office block is probably safe. But... when I look at those older doors, like the ones in my apartment building or at work, my mind just treats it like I’m about to get trapped inside, or like the whole thing is going to drop. Part of me knows that sounds pretty over the top, but that's just how my brain reacts to them.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about elevators, like the fear of the doors closing or the fear of the elevator moving, that triggers your panic?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if it's one specific part of it. It's just the whole experience, I guess. Like, I just look at the elevator and I get that feeling. I haven't really broken it down into whether it's the doors or the moving part. It's just the elevator itself that sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this fear of elevators, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't always been there. It really started about seven months ago. That was right around the time I moved into my current place—it's a fifth-floor apartment. I had a bit of a scare in the elevator there shortly after moving in, and ever since then, it's just been a problem.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having a scare in the elevator after moving into your current place, can you tell me more about what happened during that scare?\n\nPatient: Honestly, there isn't really much to tell. It wasn't some huge disaster or anything. The elevator just kind of... jerked to a stop for a few seconds and made a really loud noise. I thought I was going to be stuck in there. It started moving again almost right away, so nothing bad actually happened, but it just really rattled me. That's pretty much all there is to it, but ever since then, my brain just expects the worst every time I look at one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I've basically started planning my days around not using elevators. It's just getting in the way of things. Like, I take five flights of stairs at home every day just to avoid them. \n\nI even turned down a training workshop I was supposed to run recently because it was in a high-rise. And... I actually had to stop visiting one of my older computer students because his building doesn't really have another option besides the elevator. I just can't bring myself to get in one, so I figured I should probably come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience panic or intense fear when you think about being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's pretty much automatic. Like, as soon as I even step toward an elevator, or if I hear the doors clunk open, my chest just tightens up. It's... it's just elevators. I don't get that feeling anywhere else. I'm totally fine in other small spaces or whatever, and heights don't bother me. It's literally just elevators that do this to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid any social situations because of fear of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, my job is literally training people, so I'm pretty comfortable talking in front of groups or being evaluated. It's really just the elevator thing that's the problem. I don't worry about people judging me or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators related to being in a confined or enclosed space, or is it something specific about elevators?\n\nPatient: It's definitely just the elevators themselves. I mean, I know an elevator in a regular office block is probably safe. But... when I look at those older doors, like the ones in my apartment building or at work, my mind just treats it like I’m about to get trapped inside, or like the whole thing is going to drop. Part of me knows that sounds pretty over the top, but that's just how my brain reacts to them.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about elevators, like the fear of the doors closing or the fear of the elevator moving, that triggers your panic?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if it's one specific part of it. It's just the whole experience, I guess. Like, I just look at the elevator and I get that feeling. I haven't really broken it down into whether it's the doors or the moving part. It's just the elevator itself that sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this fear of elevators, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't always been there. It really started about seven months ago. That was right around the time I moved into my current place—it's a fifth-floor apartment. That's pretty much all there is to it, but ever since then, my brain just expects the worst every time I look at one.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really with family or friends, no. They just kind of deal with it, or we take the stairs if we're out together. But it has started affecting my work and my volunteering. \n\nI actually had to turn down a workshop assignment recently because it was being held in a high-rise building. And... well, I also volunteer teaching computer skills to seniors, and I had to stop visiting one of my students. His building is just set up in a way that basically requires using the elevator to get to his floor, and I just couldn't bring myself to do it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and volunteering, where your fear of elevators is causing problems or making it difficult for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: No, not really.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I've basically started planning my days around not using elevators. It's just getting in the way of things. Like, I take five flights of stairs at home every day just to avoid them. \n\nI even turned down a training workshop I was supposed to run recently because it was in a high-rise. And... I actually had to stop visiting one of my older computer students because his building doesn't really have another option besides the elevator. I just can't bring myself to get in one, so I figured I should probably come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience panic or intense fear when you think about being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's pretty much automatic. Like, as soon as I even step toward an elevator, or if I hear the doors clunk open, my chest just tightens up. My hands get sweaty, and I just feel this instant rush of panic before I've even had time to really think it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar panic or fear in situations other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's... it's just elevators. I don't get that feeling anywhere else. I'm totally fine in other small spaces or whatever, and heights don't bother me. It's literally just elevators that do this to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid any social situations because of fear of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, my job is literally training people, so I'm pretty comfortable talking in front of groups or being evaluated. It's really just the elevator thing that's the problem. I don't worry about people judging me or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators related to being in a confined or enclosed space, or is it something specific about elevators?\n\nPatient: It's definitely just the elevators themselves. I mean, I know an elevator in a regular office block is probably safe. But... when I look at those older doors, like the ones in my apartment building or at work, my mind just treats it like I’m about to get trapped inside, or like the whole thing is going to drop. Part of me knows that sounds pretty over the top, but that's just how my brain reacts to them.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about elevators, like the fear of the doors closing or the fear of the elevator moving, that triggers your panic?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if it's one specific part of it. It's just the whole experience, I guess. Like, I just look at the elevator and I get that feeling. I haven't really broken it down into whether it's the doors or the moving part. It's just the elevator itself that sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this fear of elevators, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't always been there. It really started about seven months ago. That was right around the time I moved into my current place—it's a fifth-floor apartment. I had a bit of a scare in the elevator there shortly after moving in, and ever since then, it's just been a problem.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having a scare in the elevator after moving into your current place, can you tell me more about what happened during that scare?\n\nPatient: Honestly, there isn't really much to tell. It wasn't some huge disaster or anything. The elevator just kind of... jerked to a stop for a few seconds and made a really loud noise. I thought I was going to be stuck in there. It started moving again almost right away, so nothing bad actually happened, but it just really rattled me. That's pretty much all there is to it, but ever since then, my brain just expects the worst every time I look at one.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really with family or friends, no. They just kind of deal with it, or we take the stairs if we're out together. But it has started affecting my work and my volunteering. \n\nI actually had to turn down a workshop assignment recently because it was being held in a high-rise building. And... well, I also volunteer teaching computer skills to seniors, and I had to stop visiting one of my students. His building is just set up in a way that basically requires using the elevator to get to his floor, and I just couldn't bring myself to do it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and volunteering, where your fear of elevators is causing problems or making it difficult for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's mostly just the work and volunteering stuff I mentioned. I mean, I live on the fifth floor, but I just take the stairs every day. It's a bit of a workout, but it doesn't stop me from getting home or anything. So yeah, outside of those specific situations, it hasn't really caused any other major problems.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant distress or anxiety when you think about the impact your elevator phobia is having on your life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm feeling significant distress about it. I mean, it's annoying, sure. It's frustrating that I had to miss that workshop and stop seeing that one student. But I don't sit around feeling super anxious or upset about the impact it's having. I just kind of work around it and get on with things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It's a bit of a workout, but it doesn't stop me from getting home or anything. So yeah, outside of those specific situations, it hasn't really caused any other major problems.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant distress or anxiety when you think about the impact your elevator phobia is having on your life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm feeling significant distress about it. I mean, it's annoying, sure. It's frustrating that I had to miss that workshop and stop seeing that one student. But I don't sit around feeling super anxious or upset about the impact it's having. I just kind of work around it and get on with things.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain your current lifestyle due to your avoidance of elevators?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I said, I just take the stairs to my apartment, and most places I go don't really require an elevator. So my day-to-day life is pretty much the same. It's just those couple of specific things with work and volunteering that have been an issue.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your fear of elevators is holding you back from pursuing things you want to do in life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I guess it stopped me from doing that one workshop, but in the grand scheme of things, I don't feel like it's holding me back from my overall goals or anything. I still do my job, I still volunteer, I just... take the stairs when I can. It's not stopping me from living my life.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I've basically started planning my days around not using elevators. It's just getting in the way of things. Like, I take five flights of stairs at home every day just to avoid them. \n\nI even turned down a training workshop I was supposed to run recently because it was in a high-rise. And... I actually had to stop visiting one of my older computer students because his building doesn't really have another option besides the elevator. I just can't bring myself to get in one, so I figured I should probably come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience panic or intense fear when you think about being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's pretty much automatic. Like, as soon as I even step toward an elevator, or if I hear the doors clunk open, my chest just tightens up. My hands get sweaty, and I just feel this instant rush of panic before I've even had time to really think it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar panic or fear in situations other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's... it's just elevators. I don't get that feeling anywhere else. I'm totally fine in other small spaces or whatever, and heights don't bother me. It's literally just elevators that do this to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid any social situations because of fear of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, my job is literally training people, so I'm pretty comfortable talking in front of groups or being evaluated. It's really just the elevator thing that's the problem. I don't worry about people judging me or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators related to being in a confined or enclosed space, or is it something specific about elevators?\n\nPatient: It's definitely just the elevators themselves. I mean, I know an elevator in a regular office block is probably safe. But... when I look at those older doors, like the ones in my apartment building or at work, my mind just treats it like I’m about to get trapped inside, or like the whole thing is going to drop. Part of me knows that sounds pretty over the top, but that's just how my brain reacts to them.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about elevators, like the fear of the doors closing or the fear of the elevator moving, that triggers your panic?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if it's one specific part of it. It's just the whole experience, I guess. Like, I just look at the elevator and I get that feeling. I haven't really broken it down into whether it's the doors or the moving part. It's just the elevator itself that sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this fear of elevators, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't always been there. It really started about seven months ago. That was right around the time I moved into my current place—it's a fifth-floor apartment. I had a bit of a scare in the elevator there shortly after moving in, and ever since then, it's just been a problem.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having a scare in the elevator after moving into your current place, can you tell me more about what happened during that scare?\n\nPatient: Honestly, there isn't really much to tell. It wasn't some huge disaster or anything. The elevator just kind of... jerked to a stop for a few seconds and made a really loud noise. I thought I was going to be stuck in there. It started moving again almost right away, so nothing bad actually happened, but it just really rattled me. That's pretty much all there is to it, but ever since then, my brain just expects the worst every time I look at one.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really with family or friends, no. They just kind of deal with it, or we take the stairs if we're out together. But it has started affecting my work and my volunteering. \n\nI actually had to turn down a workshop assignment recently because it was being held in a high-rise building. And... well, I also volunteer teaching computer skills to seniors, and I had to stop visiting one of my students. His building is just set up in a way that basically requires using the elevator to get to his floor, and I just couldn't bring myself to do it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and volunteering, where your fear of elevators is causing problems or making it difficult for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's mostly just the work and volunteering stuff I mentioned. I mean, I live on the fifth floor, but I just take the stairs every day. It's a bit of a workout, but it doesn't stop me from getting home or anything. So yeah, outside of those specific situations, it hasn't really caused any other major problems.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing significant distress or anxiety when you think about the impact your elevator phobia is having on your life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm feeling significant distress about it. I mean, it's annoying, sure. It's frustrating that I had to miss that workshop and stop seeing that one student. But I don't sit around feeling super anxious or upset about the impact it's having. I just kind of work around it and get on with things.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain your current lifestyle due to your avoidance of elevators?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Like I said, I just take the stairs to my apartment, and most places I go don't really require an elevator. So my day-to-day life is pretty much the same. It's just those couple of specific things with work and volunteering that have been an issue.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your fear of elevators is holding you back from pursuing things you want to do in life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I guess it stopped me from doing that one workshop, but in the grand scheme of things, I don't feel like it's holding me back from my overall goals or anything. I still do my job, I still volunteer, I just... take the stairs when I can. It's not stopping me from living my life.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social gatherings or events because they might be held in a location that has elevators?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I still go out with my friends and do whatever social stuff comes up. If we happen to go somewhere with an elevator, I just find the stairs. It hasn't kept me from hanging out with people or going to events.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I'm here because I've basically started planning my days around not using elevators. It's just getting in the way of things. Like, I take five flights of stairs at home every day just to avoid them. \n\nI even turned down a training workshop I was supposed to run recently because it was in a high-rise. And... I actually had to stop visiting one of my older computer students because his building doesn't really have another option besides the elevator. I just can't bring myself to get in one, so I figured I should probably come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience panic or intense fear when you think about being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's pretty much automatic. Like, as soon as I even step toward an elevator, or if I hear the doors clunk open, my chest just tightens up. My hands get sweaty, and I just feel this instant rush of panic before I've even had time to really think it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar panic or fear in situations other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's... it's just elevators. I don't get that feeling anywhere else. I'm totally fine in other small spaces or whatever, and heights don't bother me. It's literally just elevators that do this to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid any social situations because of fear of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, my job is literally training people, so I'm pretty comfortable talking in front of groups or being evaluated. It's really just the elevator thing that's the problem. I don't worry about people judging me or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators related to being in a confined or enclosed space, or is it something specific about elevators?\n\nPatient: It's definitely just the elevators themselves. I mean, I know an elevator in a regular office block is probably safe. But... when I look at those older doors, like the ones in my apartment building or at work, my mind just treats it like I’m about to get trapped inside, or like the whole thing is going to drop. Part of me knows that sounds pretty over the top, but that's just how my brain reacts to them.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about elevators, like the fear of the doors closing or the fear of the elevator moving, that triggers your panic?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if it's one specific part of it. It's just the whole experience, I guess. Like, I just look at the elevator and I get that feeling. I haven't really broken it down into whether it's the doors or the moving part. It's just the elevator itself that sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this fear of elevators, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't always been there. It really started about seven months ago. That was right around the time I moved into my current place—it's a fifth-floor apartment. I had a bit of a scare in the elevator there shortly after moving in, and ever since then, it's just been a problem.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having a scare in the elevator after moving into your current place, can you tell me more about what happened during that scare?\n\nPatient: Honestly, there isn't really much to tell. It wasn't some huge disaster or anything. The elevator just kind of... jerked to a stop for a few seconds and made a really loud noise. I thought I was going to be stuck in there. It started moving again almost right away, so nothing bad actually happened, but it just really rattled me. That's pretty much all there is to it, but ever since then, my brain just expects the worst every time I look at one.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really with family or friends, no. They just kind of deal with it, or we take the stairs if we're out together. But it has started affecting my work and my volunteering. \n\nI actually had to turn down a workshop assignment recently because it was being held in a high-rise building. And... well, I also volunteer teaching computer skills to seniors, and I had to stop visiting one of my students. His building is just set up in a way that basically requires using the elevator to get to his floor, and I just couldn't bring myself to do it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work and volunteering, where your fear of elevators is causing problems or making it difficult for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I just kind of work around it and get on with things.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain your current lifestyle due to your avoidance of elevators?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's pretty much automatic. Like, as soon as I even step toward an elevator, or if I hear the doors clunk open, my chest just tightens up. My hands get sweaty, and I just feel this instant rush of panic before I've even had time to really think it through.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience similar panic or fear in situations other than being in an elevator?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's... it's just elevators. I don't get that feeling anywhere else. I'm totally fine in other small spaces or whatever, and heights don't bother me. It's literally just elevators that do this to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid any social situations because of fear of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, my job is literally training people, so I'm pretty comfortable talking in front of groups or being evaluated. It's really just the elevator thing that's the problem. I don't worry about people judging me or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of elevators related to being in a confined or enclosed space, or is it something specific about elevators?\n\nPatient: It's definitely just the elevators themselves. I mean, I know an elevator in a regular office block is probably safe. Part of me knows that sounds pretty over the top, but that's just how my brain reacts to them.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about elevators, like the fear of the doors closing or the fear of the elevator moving, that triggers your panic?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if it's one specific part of it. It's just the whole experience, I guess. Like, I just look at the elevator and I get that feeling. I haven't really broken it down into whether it's the doors or the moving part. It's just the elevator itself that sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had this fear of elevators, or is it something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't always been there. It really started about seven months ago. That was right around the time I moved into my current place—it's a fifth-floor apartment. I had a bit of a scare in the elevator there shortly after moving in, and ever since then, it's just been a problem.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned having a scare in the elevator after moving into your current place, can you tell me more about what happened during that scare?\n\nPatient: Honestly, there isn't really much to tell. That's pretty much all there is to it, but ever since then, my brain just expects the worst every time I look at one.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of elevators affected your relationships with family or friends in any way?\n\nPatient: Not really with family or friends, no. They just kind of deal with it, or we take the stairs if we're out together. It's just those couple of specific things with work and volunteering that have been an issue.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your fear of elevators is holding you back from pursuing things you want to do in life?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I guess it stopped me from doing that one workshop, but in the grand scheme of things, I don't feel like it's holding me back from my overall goals or anything.
